# WEEK 1 — DAYS 1–7


---

# WEEK 1 STUDY PLAN: FOUNDATIONS OF CONTACT, IDENTITY, AND V2 SYNTAX

---

## DAY 1: Formal Greetings, Self-Identity, and the First Person Singular (*ich bin*)

### 1. Day Objective

The learner will be able to initiate a formal conversation using *Guten Tag*, introduce themselves using the first-person singular copula (*ich bin*), and establish a stable, default S-V-O word order while respecting the fundamental Verb-Second (V2) rule of German syntax.

### 2. Main Curriculum Lesson Content

- **INTRODUCE & UNDERSTAND:** The teacher models the formal greeting *Guten Tag* and the identity statement *Ich bin [Name]* [Unit 1]. The student learns that in a German main clause, the conjugated verb is the absolute anchor and must sit in Position 2.
- **RECOGNIZE:** The learner listens to native recordings and identifies the exact boundary where *ich* ends and *bin*begins in connected speech, noticing the glottal stop (*Knacklaut*) preceding the vowel-initial *ich* [Unit 1].
- **RETRIEVE:** The learner retrieves their own name and basic English cognates (such as *Name*) to populate the slots.
- **CONTROLLED USE:** Matching exercises where scrambled pronouns (*ich*, *Sie*) are snapped together with correct present-tense forms of *sein* (*bin*, *sind*).
- **SENTENCE BUILDING:** See Section 10.
- **CONTEXTUAL USE:** Greeting a partner formally and stating one's name under un-speeded classroom conditions [Unit 1].
- **REAL-LIFE USE:** Approaching an administrator at a training desk and providing identity details in German [Unit 1].
- **REVISIT:** Spaced review of the spelling of one's own name at the end of the study session.

### 3. Daily German Core Items

- **Guten Tag** (Good day / Formal greeting / Interaction Formula) [Day 1 core]
- **Ja / Nein** (Yes / No / Interaction Formula) [Day 1 core]
- **Danke** (Thank you / Interaction Formula) [Day 1 core]

### 4. Pronunciation Practice

- **Focus:** The German initial **"W" sound (/v/)** vs. the English /w/ glide default [Unit 1].
- **Progression:**
  1. *Sound:* /v/ (Voiced labiodental fricative—top teeth gently touching the lower lip, producing a distinct buzzing friction sound).
  2. *Syllable:* /ve:ɐ̯/
  3. *Word:* *wer* [ve:ɐ̯] (Who)
  4. *Phrase:* *Wer ist das?* [ve:ɐ̯ ɪst das]
  5. *Sentence:* *Guten Tag. Wer ist das?*
  6. *Connected Speech:* *Guten Tag, wer ist das?* (Slide naturally with immediate vocal cord vibration on /v/).

### 5. Minimum Theory/Explanation

- **The Verb-Second (V2) Rule:** German sentences are built like a train with numbered cars. The conjugated verb is the engine, and it is **permanently locked into Car 2 (Position 2)**. For beginners, the simplest default starting template places the subject in Car 1, creating a Subject-Verb-Object (S-V-O) pattern [Unit 1]: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c} \text{Car 1: Subject} & \text{Car 2: Verb (Locked)} & \text{Car 3: Modifier/Name} \ \hline \text{Ich} & \text{bin} & \text{David.} \ \text{Sie} & \text{sind} & \text{Herr Wagner.} \end{array}$$

### 6. Listening Practice

- **Bottom-Up Decoding:** Listen to five native German speakers pronouncing the sentence *Ich bin [Name]*. Identify how the voiceless palatal fricative ("ich-laut" /ç/) is produced cleanly as a "hissing cat" sound without slipping into the English "sh" (/ʃ/) or a throat-clearing "ach-laut" (/x/) [Unit 1, 328].
- **Gap Detection:** Listen to a recording with background cafe noise. Circle the word *bin* when heard in a sequence of rapid self-introductions.

### 7. Speaking Practice

- **Shadowing Exercise:** Listen to a native audio clip of *Guten Tag. Ich bin Alex.* and repeat it immediately, matching the rhythm and stress of the speaker.
- **Controlled Output:** The student is shown visual nameplates (e.g., *Herr Schmidt*, *Frau Weber*) and must state: *Ich bin Herr Schmidt* or *Ich bin Frau Weber* with crisp, clear articulation.

### 8. Reading Practice

- The student scans a simplified business registry sheet containing four entries:
  - *Guten Tag. Ich bin Maria.*
  - *Guten Tag. Ich bin John.*
- The learner highlights the verb *bin* in each sentence to visually reinforce its position as the second element in the clause [Unit 1].

### 9. Writing Practice

- **Controlled Copying:** The learner copies the following frames into their notebook by hand, paying strict attention to capitalizing the sentence starters and names:
  1. `Guten Tag. Ich bin [Your Name].`
  2. `Danke.`

### 10. Mandatory Daily Sentence-Building

- **Task:** Scrambled Word Ordering.
- **Input Blocks:** `bin` / `Ich` / `Guten Tag` / `Anna`
- **Execution:** The learner must physically reorder these blocks to create a grammatically correct V2 sentence:
  - *Guten Tag. Ich bin Anna.*
- **Substitution Drill:** Replace the name *Anna* with the formal titles *Herr Schmidt* and *Frau Müller* to produce:
  - *Ich bin Herr Schmidt.*
  - *Ich bin Frau Müller.*

### 11. Retrieval/Review

- As Day 1 is the baseline, review is focused on the phonological distinction between English /w/ and German /v/ using the diagnostic word *wer* [ve:ɐ̯] [Unit 1].

### 12. Practical Germany Task

- **The Reception desk check-in:** The learner roleplays walking up to a reception desk at a local community center in Germany [Unit 1]. They must formally greet the clerk, state their own name using the correct S-V-O baseline, and express gratitude:
  - *Learner:* "Guten Tag. Ich bin John. Danke." [Unit 1]

### 13. Communication-Repair Practice

- If the receptionist (the teacher) looks confused, the learner immediately applies the Day 3 Core repair strategy: **"Wie bitte?"** [Day 3 core] to prompt a repetition, ensuring they remain entirely in German without reverting to English.

### 14. Native-Response/Interaction Practice

- *Native (Clerk):* "Guten Tag. Wer ist das?"
- *Learner:* "Guten Tag. Ich bin John."
- *Native (Clerk):* "Ah, Herr John. Ja. Danke."
- *Learner:* "Danke."

### 15. Optional Interest-Driven German Exposure

- Listen to a short German pop song or look at a children's cartoon. Focus entirely on recognizing the words *Guten Tag*, *ja*, *nein*, and *ich* in the audio track.

### 16. Mastery Check

- **Can-Do Checklist:**
  - Can formally greet an interlocutor (*Guten Tag*) [Day 1 core].
  - Can state their name using *Ich bin [Name]* with the verb locked in Position 2 [Unit 1].
  - Can pronounce the German "W" (/v/) without English "w" slurs [Unit 1].

---

### WEEK 1, DAY 1 TIME BUDGET ALLOCATION (Total: \~4 Hours)

- **A. Daily German Core:** 15 minutes (Focusing on *Guten Tag*, *Ja/Nein*, *Danke*) [Day 1 core].
- **B. Main Curriculum Lesson:** 45 minutes (V2 rules, *sein* conjugation, pronoun-verb matching) [Unit 1].
- **C. Intensive Practice & Drills:** 2.5 hours (Spaced retrieval of sentence templates, writing practice, shadowing native audio clips, spelling exercises).
- **D. Real-Life German / Interest-Driven Exposure:** 30 minutes (Listening to German music, practicing self-talk in the mirror) [3Third, 322].

---

## DAY 2: Informal Greetings, *sein* Conjugation, and formal address (*Sie sind*)

### 1. Day Objective

The learner will be able to distinguish between formal (*Sie*) and informal (*du*) address protocols, conjugate *sein* for the formal address (*Sie sind*), and maintain perfect V2 syntax in simple identity assertions.

### 2. Main Curriculum Lesson Content

- **INTRODUCE & UNDERSTAND:** The teacher introduces the informal greeting *Hallo* and the formal copula *Sie sind* [Unit 1]. The student learns the social boundaries between formal and informal registers in German-speaking countries [Unit 1].
- **RECOGNIZE:** The learner reviews spoken scenarios and identifies whether the speaker is utilizing the formal *Sie*or the informal *du* register [Unit 1].
- **RETRIEVE:** Retrieval of *Guten Tag*, *Ich bin*, and *Danke* from Day 1 to form conversational chains.
- **CONTROLLED USE:** Conjugating *sein* based on pronoun prompts (*ich* \\(\rightarrow\\) *bin*, *Sie* \\(\rightarrow\\) *sind*).
- **SENTENCE BUILDING:** See Section 10.
- **CONTEXTUAL USE:** Exchanging roles in a simulated workspace lobby, alternating between introducing oneself and confirming the partner's formal identity [Unit 1].
- **REAL-LIFE USE:** Confirming the identity of a professional training coordinator or HR administrator on Day 1 [Unit 1].
- **REVISIT:** Quick pronunciation check of the "ich-laut" (/ç/) before concluding the session.

### 3. Daily German Core Items

- **Tschüss** (Bye / Informal parting / Interaction Formula) [Day 2 core]

### 4. Pronunciation Practice

- **Focus:** The German **"S" sound (/z/ vs. /s/)** and the tense **long vowel /i:/** in *Sie* [Unit 1].
- **Progression:**
  1. *Sound:* /z/ (Voiced alveolar sibilant—pronounced like a buzzing English "z" when standing before a vowel at the start of a word).
  2. *Syllable:* /zi:/
  3. *Word:* *Sie* [zi:] (You—formal)
  4. *Phrase:* *Sie sind...* [zi: zɪnt]
  5. *Sentence:* *Guten Tag, Sie sind Herr Wagner.*
  6. *Connected Speech:* *Guten Tag, Herr Wagner, Sie sind da.* (Notice how the first "s" in *sind* also buzzes: [zɪnt], while the final "s" in *Tschüss* is voiceless: [tʃy\:s] [Day 2 core]).

### 5. Minimum Theory/Explanation

- **The Register Split:** German divides "you" into two distinct social categories [Unit 1]:
  - **Sie (Capitalized):** The formal "you." Used with strangers, teachers, doctors, and professional supervisors [Unit 1]. The corresponding verb form of *sein* is **sind** [Unit 1].
  - **du (Lowercase):** The informal "you." Used with family, close friends, and children. (We focus on mastering the formal *Sie sind* first to ensure professional safety).
  - Both forms keep the verb locked securely in **Position 2** [Unit 1]: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c} \text{Position 1} & \text{Position 2 (Verb)} & \text{Position 3} \ \hline \text{Sie} & \text{sind} & \text{Herr Schmidt.} \end{array}$$

### 6. Listening Practice

- **Bottom-Up Decoding:** Listen to three different dialogues recorded in a German office corridor [Unit 11]. Identify whether the speakers say *Sie sind* or *Ich bin* by checking the corresponding box on a worksheet.
- **Phoneme Identification:** Listen to a speaker saying *sind* vs. *bist*. Focus on isolating the voiced starting sound /z/ in *sind* vs. the bilabial stop /b/ in *bist*.

### 7. Speaking Practice

- **Substitution Drills:** The teacher shows a photo of a person with the label *Herr Wagner*. The learner says: *Sie sind Herr Wagner.* If shown a self-pointing icon, the learner says: *Ich bin David.*
- **Liaison Practice:** Practice sliding *Guten Tag* and *Sie sind* together as a single fluid phonetic unit: /gu\:tən ta\:k zi: zɪnt/ [Unit 11].

### 8. Reading Practice

- The student reads a simple, three-line receptionist log [Unit 11]:
  - *A: Guten Tag. Ich bin Herr Wagner.*
  - *B: Guten Tag. Ja, Sie sind Herr Wagner. Danke.*
- The learner draws a circle around the formal pronoun *Sie* and the verb *sind* to confirm their parsing ability.

### 9. Writing Practice

- **Guided Writing:** The learner writes two parallel sentences in their workbook introducing themselves and acknowledging their partner:
  1. `Ich bin John.`
  2. `Sie sind Herr Schmidt.`

### 10. Mandatory Daily Sentence-Building

- **Task:** Pronoun-Verb Alignment.
- **Execution:** Fill in the missing verb (*bin* or *sind*) to match the subject pronoun, ensuring correct V2 syntax:
  - *Ich \_\_\_\_\_\_ Herr Wagner.* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Ich ****bin**** Herr Wagner.*
  - *Sie \_\_\_\_\_\_ Frau Müller.* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Sie ****sind**** Frau Müller.*
- **Expansion:** Prefix the sentence with a greeting:
  - *Guten Tag. Sie sind Frau Müller.*

### 11. Retrieval/Review

- The learner retrieves *Guten Tag* and *Danke* from Day 1 to form a complete greeting chain before attempting today's practical task.

### 12. Practical Germany Task

- **The HR check-in:** The learner roleplays arriving at a German vocational school roster verification [Unit 11]. They approach the instructor, formally introduce themselves, and confirm the instructor's identity using the formal register:
  - *Learner:* "Guten Tag. Ich bin John. Sie sind Herr Schmidt? Danke." [Unit 11]

### 13. Communication-Repair Practice

- If the instructor speaks too rapidly or mumbles, the learner deploys their Day 3 Core repair formula: **"Wie bitte?"**[Day 3 core] with a polite, rising intonation to request a slow repetition.

### 14. Native-Response/Interaction Practice

- *Native (Instructor):* "Guten Tag. Sind Sie Herr John?"
- *Learner:* "Ja, ich bin John. Und Sie sind Herr Schmidt?"
- *Native (Instructor):* "Richtig. Danke."
- *Learner:* "Danke. Tschüss!" [Day 2 core]

### 15. Optional Interest-Driven German Exposure

- Find a brief online video clip of a formal German interview. Listen specifically for the formal word *Sie* and the verb *sind* to notice how native speakers stress these elements [Unit 5].

### 16. Mastery Check

- **Can-Do Checklist:**
  - Can use the formal greeting *Guten Tag* and the informal parting *Tschüss* appropriately [Day 1, 2 core].
  - Can construct a complete, grammatically correct formal sentence using *Sie sind [Name]* [Unit 1].
  - Can distinguish between the voiced initial /z/ in *Sie* and the voiceless terminal /s/ in *Tschüss* [Day 2 core].

---

### WEEK 1, DAY 2 TIME BUDGET ALLOCATION (Total: \~4 Hours)

- **A. Daily German Core:** 20 minutes (Reviewing *Guten Tag*, *Danke*, adding *Tschüss*) [Day 1, 2 core].
- **B. Main Curriculum Lesson:** 40 minutes (Formal register split, *Sie sind* conjugation, V2 position checking) [Unit 1].
- **C. Intensive Practice & Drills:** 2.5 hours (Roleplaying reception interactions, practicing phonetic sibilants /z/ vs /s/, sentence-building blocks).
- **D. Real-Life German / Interest-Driven Exposure:** 30 minutes (Watching native dialogues online, practicing saying *Tschüss* with proper terminal voicelessness) [3Third, 322].

---

## DAY 3: Polite Requests, Language Profiles, and the Core Repair Chunks

### 1. Day Objective

The learner will be able to state their linguistic profile using the irregular verb *sprechen* (*ich spreche*), ask a formal partner if they speak English, and deploy essential communication-repair chunks to manage conversational breakdowns.

### 2. Main Curriculum Lesson Content

- **INTRODUCE & UNDERSTAND:** The teacher introduces the verb *sprechen* and the formal question structure *Sprechen Sie Englisch?* [Day 3 core]. The student learns how to state their language capability and request help politely [Day 3, 9 core].
- **RECOGNIZE:** The learner listens to short interactions and identifies when a speaker requests clarification using *Wie bitte?* or *Sprechen Sie Englisch?* [Day 3 core].
- **RETRIEVE:** Retrieval of *Guten Tag*, *Ich bin*, and *sein* conjugations from Days 1–2.
- **CONTROLLED USE:** Converting declarative sentences into questions by swapping pronoun and verb positions.
- **SENTENCE BUILDING:** See Section 10.
- **CONTEXTUAL USE:** Mingle activity where learners ask classmates about their language abilities [Unit 15].
- **REAL-LIFE USE:** Approaching an information counter and asking the agent if they speak English [Day 3 core].
- **REVISIT:** Spelling verification of key terms before finishing the lesson.

### 3. Daily German Core Items

- **Wie bitte?** (Pardon? / What did you say? / Interaction Formula) [Day 3 core]
- **Sprechen Sie Englisch?** (Do you speak English? / Sentence Frame) [Day 3 core]
- **Ein bisschen** (A little / Modifier) [Day 3 core]

### 4. Pronunciation Practice

- **Focus:** The voiceless palatal fricative **"ich-laut" (/ç/)** in *ich*, *spreche*, and *bisschen* [Unit 1, 318].
- **Progression:**
  1. *Sound:* /ç/ (Open mouth as if to smile, push the tongue forward against the lower teeth, and blow air out to make a "hissing cat" sound).
  2. *Syllable:* /sprɛçə/
  3. *Word:* *spreche* [ˈʃpʁɛçə]
  4. *Phrase:* *ich spreche* [ɪç ˈʃpʁɛçə]
  5. *Sentence:* *Ich spreche ein bisschen Deutsch.* [ɪç ˈʃpʁɛçə aɪn ˈbɪsçən dɔʏtʃ]
  6. *Connected Speech:* *Sprechen Sie Englisch? — Ja, ich spreche ein bisschen Deutsch.*

### 5. Minimum Theory/Explanation

- **Forming Yes/No Questions:** To ask a question that requires a "yes" or "no" answer in German, we swap the subject and the verb. The conjugated verb moves to **Position 1**, and the subject pronoun follows in **Position 2** [Unit 1]: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c} \text{Position 1 (Verb)} & \text{Position 2 (Subject)} & \text{Position 3 (Object/Language)} \ \hline \text{Sprechen} & \text{Sie} & \text{Englisch?} \ \text{Sind} & \text{Sie} & \text{Herr Wagner?} \end{array}$$ *Notice that the verb still anchors the early part of the sentence, maintaining strict syntactic organization.*

### 6. Listening Practice

- **Bottom-Up Decoding:** Listen to a native speaker pronouncing the sentence *Ich spreche ein bisschen Deutsch.* [Day 3 core]. Count how many times you hear the "ich-laut" (/ç/) phoneme (Answer: 3 times—in *ich*, *spreche*, and *bisschen*).
- **Tone Detection:** Listen to three native questions and statements. Identify whether the intonation rises at the end (for questions) or falls (for statements).

### 7. Speaking Practice

- **The Repetition Loop:** Practice repeating the repair formula *Wie bitte?* with a natural, polite rising intonation to signal that you need a partner to repeat their statement [Day 3 core].
- **Oral Substitution:** Translate on the fly:
  - "I speak English." \\(\rightarrow\\) *Ich spreche Englisch.*
  - "Do you speak English?" \\(\rightarrow\\) *Sprechen Sie Englisch?* [Day 3 core]

### 8. Reading Practice

- The student scans a short printed dialogue from a help desk:
  - *A: Guten Tag. Sprechen Sie Englisch?*
  - *B: Nein. Ich spreche Deutsch.*
  - *A: Wie bitte?*
  - *B: Ich spreche ein bisschen Englisch.*
- The learner underlines all instances of the verb *sprechen* and labels their syntactic positions (1 or 2).

### 9. Writing Practice

- **Guided Composition:** Write a short personal script for a first contact situation at an office:
  - `Guten Tag. Ich bin John.`
  - `Sprechen Sie Englisch?`
  - `Ich spreche ein bisschen Deutsch.`

### 10. Mandatory Daily Sentence-Building

- **Task:** Sentence Transformation (Declarative to Question).
- **Declarative:** *Sie sind Herr Schmidt.*
- **Question:** \\(\rightarrow\\) *Sind Sie Herr Schmidt?*
- **Declarative:** *Sie sprechen Englisch.*
- **Question:** \\(\rightarrow\\) *Sprechen Sie Englisch?* [Day 3 core]
- **Mini-Dialogue Creation:** Match the questions to answers:
  - *Sprechen Sie Englisch?* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Ja, ein bisschen.* [Day 3 core]

### 11. Retrieval/Review

- Retrieve the formal greetings (*Guten Tag*) and name statements (*Ich bin*) from previous days to create a coherent dialogue chain before attempting the practical task.

### 12. Practical Germany Task

- **The Info Desk Encounter:** The learner roleplays approaching an information desk at a German transit station [Day 8 core]. They must greet the clerk formally, state their own identity, inquire if the clerk speaks English, and gracefully manage the response using *ein bisschen*:
  - *Learner:* "Guten Tag. Ich bin John. Sprechen Sie Englisch? — Ein bisschen. — Danke!" [Days 1, 3 core]

### 13. Communication-Repair Practice

- If the clerk replies with a rapid, incomprehensible string of German, the learner must immediately deploy: **"Wie bitte? Ich verstehe nicht."** [Day 3 core] to slow down the interaction without falling silent.

### 14. Native-Response/Interaction Practice

- *Native (Clerk):* "Guten Tag. Sprechen Sie Deutsch?"
- *Learner:* "Wie bitte?" [Day 3 core]
- *Native (Clerk):* "Sprechen... Sie... Deutsch?"
- *Learner:* "Ich spreche ein bisschen Deutsch. Sprechen Sie Englisch?" [Day 3 core]
- *Native (Clerk):* "Ja, ich spreche Englisch."
- *Learner:* "Danke!" [Day 1 core]

### 15. Optional Interest-Driven German Exposure

- Watch a short clip of a German news broadcast or public interview. Focus on spotting the words *Sprechen Sie* and notice how native speakers naturally reduce the pronoun *Sie* in connected speech.

### 16. Mastery Check

- **Can-Do Checklist:**
  - Can ask "Sprechen Sie Englisch?" with accurate verb-first question syntax [Day 3 core].
  - Can state "Ich spreche ein bisschen Deutsch" with correct pronunciation of the "ich-laut" (/ç/) [Day 3 core].
  - Can deploy "Wie bitte?" naturally to resolve communication barriers [Day 3 core].

---

### WEEK 1, DAY 3 TIME BUDGET ALLOCATION (Total: \~4 Hours)

- **A. Daily German Core:** 20 minutes (Reviewing greetings, adding *Wie bitte?*, *Sprechen Sie Englisch?*, *Ein bisschen*) [Day 3 core].
- **B. Main Curriculum Lesson:** 40 minutes (Yes/No question formation, *sprechen* conjugation, register checks) [Unit 15].
- **C. Intensive Practice & Drills:** 2.5 hours (Drilling the "ich-laut" palatal sibilant, practicing question-answer conversions, roleplaying desk interactions).
- **D. Real-Life German / Interest-Driven Exposure:** 30 minutes (Listening to German radio, practicing saying "ein bisschen" while keeping tongue pushed against lower teeth) [3Third, 322].

---

## DAY 4: Apologies, Personal Origin, and the Preposition *aus*

### 1. Day Objective

The learner will be able to state their country of origin using *Ich komme aus...*, ask an interlocutor about their origin (*Woher kommen Sie?*), and deploy polite formal apologies (*Entschuldigung*) in communication.

### 2. Main Curriculum Lesson Content

- **INTRODUCE & UNDERSTAND:** The teacher introduces the verb *kommen* and the geographic preposition *aus*[Unit 3]. The student learns that *aus* indicates origin and is used as a fixed lexical chunk to express country of origin [Unit 3].
- **RECOGNIZE:** The learner listens to short recordings of travelers arriving at an airport and identifies their countries of origin by matching the audio to pictures [Unit 3].
- **RETRIEVE:** Retrieval of greetings and language capability profiles from previous days.
- **CONTROLLED USE:** Root-and-tail grammar drills where the student appends the correct suffix to *komm-* (*ich komme*, *Sie kommen*).
- **SENTENCE BUILDING:** See Section 10.
- **CONTEXTUAL USE:** Mingle activity ("The Cocktail Party") where students interview peers about their origin [Unit 2].
- **REAL-LIFE USE:** Completing a basic entry profile or arriving at a lodging desk in Germany and stating where you are from [Unit 3].
- **REVISIT:** Reviewing the position of the verb *kommen* in both statements and questions.

### 3. Daily German Core Items

- **Entschuldigung** (Excuse me / Apology / Interaction Formula) [Day 4 core]

### 4. Pronunciation Practice

- **Focus:** The vocalic **"h"** in *Woher* and the diphthong **"au"** in *aus* [Unit 3].
- **Progression:**
  1. *Sound:* /aʊ/ (Open the mouth wide on "a" and round lips quickly into "u", sounding like the "ou" in "house").
  2. *Syllable:* /aʊs/
  3. *Word:* *aus* [aʊs]
  4. *Phrase:* *aus England* [aʊs ˈɪŋlant]
  5. *Sentence:* *Ich komme aus England.*
  6. *Connected Speech:* *Entschuldigung, woher kommen Sie? — Ich komme aus England.* (Ensure *Woher* contains a deep, warm breathy puff of air from the chest: [vo:ˈhe:ɐ̯] [Unit 3]).

### 5. Minimum Theory/Explanation

- **Verb Inflection (Regular Suffixes):** Regular German verbs are conjugated by stripping the infinitive ending **-en** to find the verb stem, and adding personal endings [Unit 3]:
  - *ich* \\(\rightarrow\\) adds **-e** (*ich komme*) [Unit 3]
  - *Sie* \\(\rightarrow\\) adds **-en** (*Sie kommen*) [Unit 3]
- **W-Question vs. Answer Syntax:**
  - In a W-Question (*Woher*), the question word sits in Position 1, the verb in Position 2, and the subject in Position 3 [Unit 3]: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c} \text{Pos 1: W-Word} & \text{Pos 2: Verb} & \text{Pos 3: Subject} \ \hline \text{Woher} & \text{kommen} & \text{Sie?} \end{array}$$
  - The answer reverts to the S-V-O default, keeping the verb in Position 2 [Unit 2]: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c} \text{Pos 1: Subject} & \text{Pos 2: Verb} & \text{Pos 3: Prepositional Modifier} \ \hline \text{Ich} & \text{komme} & \text{aus Kanada.} \end{array}$$

### 6. Listening Practice

- **Bottom-Up Decoding:** Listen to three different speakers stating their origin. Focus on identifying the country noun and how the terminal consonant of *aus* links with the starting vowel of countries like *Italien* (*aus-Italien* [aʊs iˈta\:liən]).
- **Gist Extraction:** Listen to a rapid audio track. Identify whether the speaker uses a formal greeting (*Guten Tag*) or an informal greeting (*Hallo*) before stating their origin [Unit 1].

### 7. Speaking Practice

- **Substitution Drills:** The teacher displays a country flag. The learner says: *Ich komme aus England* or *Ich komme aus Spanien.*
- **Intonation Practice:** Practice pronouncing *Entschuldigung* [Day 4 core] with a polite, falling intonation to catch someone's attention in a crowded public transit hub.

### 8. Reading Practice

- The student reads a basicprinted participant roster from a language school:
  - *Name: John Wagner. Herkunft: England.*
  - *Name: Maria Sato. Herkunft: Japan.*
- The student translates these profiles into spoken sentences: *John Wagner kommt aus England* and *Maria Sato kommt aus Japan*.

### 9. Writing Practice

- **Guided Writing:** The learner writes a short formal self-introduction paragraph:
  - `Guten Tag. Ich bin John.`
  - `Ich komme aus England.`
  - `Ich spreche Englisch.`

### 10. Mandatory Daily Sentence-Building

- **Task:** Combining Old and New Vocabulary.
- **Execution:** Combine identity pronouns (*ich*, *Sie*), greetings, and origin phrases to build simple, accurate dialogues:
  - *Guten Tag. Ich bin John. Ich komme aus England.*
  - *Entschuldigung, wer sind Sie?* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Ich bin Herr Schmidt.*
  - *Woher kommen Sie, Herr Schmidt?* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Ich komme aus Deutschland.*

### 11. Retrieval/Review

- Review the pronunciation of the "ich-laut" (/ç/) in *ich* and *spreche* to ensure it remains stable when adding the new origin sentences.

### 12. Practical Germany Task

- **The Lodging Desk Arrival:** The learner roleplays checking in at a German guesthouse or hostel. They must formally greet the clerk, apologize politely using *Entschuldigung* to catch their attention, state their name, and answer the origin inquiry [Day 4 core, Unit 3]:
  - *Learner:* "Entschuldigung. Guten Tag. Ich bin John. Ich komme aus England. Danke!" [Days 1, 4 core, Unit 3]

### 13. Communication-Repair Practice

- If the clerk asks an unexpected question about documents, the learner deploys: **"Wie bitte? Ich verstehe nicht."**[Day 3 core] to prompt a simplification in German.

### 14. Native-Response/Interaction Practice

- *Native (Clerk):* "Guten Tag. Woher kommen Sie?"
- *Learner:* "Ich komme aus England. Und Sie?" [Unit 3]
- *Native (Clerk):* "Ich komme aus Deutschland. Danke."
- *Learner:* "Danke. Tschüss!" [Days 1, 2 core]

### 15. Optional Interest-Driven German Exposure

- Listen to an interview with a German public figure or athlete. Listen specifically for how they use *Ich komme aus...*and notice the vowel transitions in the word *aus*.

### 16. Mastery Check

- **Can-Do Checklist:**
  - Can catch an interlocutor's attention formally using *Entschuldigung* [Day 4 core].
  - Can ask "Woher kommen Sie?" with correct V2 question syntax [Unit 3].
  - Can state their origin using *Ich komme aus [Country]* without grammatical suffix errors [Unit 3].

---

### WEEK 1, DAY 4 TIME BUDGET ALLOCATION (Total: \~4 Hours)

- **A. Daily German Core:** 15 minutes (Reviewing previous items, adding *Entschuldigung*) [Day 4 core].
- **B. Main Curriculum Lesson:** 45 minutes (Regular suffix endings on *kommen*, W-question word order, *aus*preposition mapping) [Unit 3].
- **C. Intensive Practice & Drills:** 2.5 hours (Shadowing origin recordings, practicing the "au" diphthong /aʊ/, executing substitution drills).
- **D. Real-Life German / Interest-Driven Exposure:** 30 minutes (Listening to German dialogues, practicing self-narration about country of origin in the mirror) [3Third, 322].

---

## DAY 5: Parting Protocols, The German Alphabet, and Alphanumeric spelling

### 1. Day Objective

The learner will be able to exit a conversation formally using *Auf Wiedersehen*, spell out their first name using the German alphabet, and decode spoken spelled names.

### 2. Main Curriculum Lesson Content

- **INTRODUCE & UNDERSTAND:** The teacher introduces the formal parting *Auf Wiedersehen* and the German alphabet letter names [Day 5 core, Unit 3]. The student learns how spelling names is essential for local administrative registrations (*Anmeldung*) [Unit 3].
- **RECOGNIZE:** The learner listens to spelling coordinates and writes down the spoken letters, distinguishing tricky letter contrasts [Unit 3].
- **RETRIEVE:** Retrieval of formal greetings and personal origin statements to conclude conversations politely.
- **CONTROLLED USE:** Spelling out given German city names (*M-U-E-N-C-H-E-N*) using correct letter names [Unit 3].
- **SENTENCE BUILDING:** See Section 10.
- **CONTEXTUAL USE:** Partner work where learners register each other by spelling and transcribing names [Unit 3].
- **REAL-LIFE USE:** Roleplaying a registration clerk checking your passport details and transcribing your spelled-out name [Unit 3].
- **REVISIT:** Spaced retrieval of the spelling of tricky letters before concluding the study day.

### 3. Daily German Core Items

- **Auf Wiedersehen** (Goodbye / Formal parting / Interaction Formula) [Day 5 core]

### 4. Pronunciation Practice

- **Focus:** Tricky German **alphabet letter contrasts** (*E* [e:] vs. *I* [i:], *A* [a:] vs. *H* [ha:], *J* [jot] vs. *Y* [ˈʏpsilɔn], and *Z*[tsɛt]) [Unit 3].
- **Progression:**
  1. *Sound:* /ts/ (Alveolar affricate—pronounced like the "ts" at the end of the English word "cats" but placed at the start of a syllable).
  2. *Syllable:* /tsɛt/
  3. *Word:* *Zet* [tsɛt] (Letter Z)
  4. *Phrase:* *Buchstabieren Sie...* [bu\:xʃtabi:ʁən zi:]
  5. *Sentence:* *Buchstabieren Sie das, bitte!* [bu\:xʃtabi:ʁən zi: das ˈbɪtə]
  6. *Connected Speech:* *Ich heiße John. Buchstabieren Sie das, bitte! — J-O-H-N.* [Unit 3]

### 5. Minimum Theory/Explanation

- **The Phonic Spelling Code:** German letter names differ significantly from English. Absolute spelling precision is mandatory for German paperwork [Unit 3]. Tricky letters to memorize:
  - **E** is pronounced **[e:]** (sounds like English "ay") [Unit 3].
  - **I** is pronounced **[i:]** (sounds like English "ee") [Unit 3].
  - **J** is pronounced **[jot]** (sounds like "yot") [Unit 3].
  - **W** is pronounced **[ve:]** [Unit 1].
  - **V** is pronounced **[fao]** [Unit 1].
  - **Z** is pronounced **[tsɛt]**.

### 6. Listening Practice

- **Odd-One-Out Discrimination:** Listen to three spelled letters (e.g., *E—E—I*). Identify which letter was different (the third letter, *I* [i:]) [Unit 3].
- **Alphanumeric Transcription:** Listen to a recording of an administrative clerk spelling out four German names. Write down the letters exactly as they are spoken on a grid worksheet.

### 7. Speaking Practice

- **The Spelling Challenge:** Spell your own first and last name aloud in German without looking at a cheat sheet.
- **Parting Fluency:** Practice saying *Auf Wiedersehen* [Day 5 core] with a smooth, connected breath and standard formal intonation: /aʊf ˈvi\:dɐˌze:ən/.

### 8. Reading Practice

- The student reads an official registration form layout [Unit 3]:
  - *Name: Alex Mercer*
  - *Buchstabieren: A-L-E-X M-E-R-C-E-R*
- The student reads the letters aloud in German to verify accuracy.

### 9. Writing Practice

- **Controlled Spelling Composition:** The student writes down five German city names spelled aloud by the teacher:
  1. `B-E-R-L-I-N`
  2. `K-O-E-L-N`
  3. `F-R-A-N-K-F-U-R-T`

### 10. Mandatory Daily Sentence-Building

- **Task:** Scrambled Questions and Spelled Answers.
- **Execution:** Rearrange scrambled words to formulate spelling requests, and answer them by spelling a name:
  - `bitte` / `Buchstabieren` / `Sie` / `das` $\rightarrow$ *Buchstabieren Sie das, bitte!* [Unit 3]
  - `heiße` / `ich` / `David` $\rightarrow$ *Ich heiße David. D-A-V-I-D.* [Unit 2, 3]

### 11. Retrieval/Review

- Retrieve the personal origin templates (*Ich komme aus...*) and greetings from previous days to build a complete social introduction block.

### 12. Practical Germany Task

- **The Bürgeramt Registration:** The learner roleplays arriving at a local registration office in Germany (*Bürgeramt*) [Unit 3]. They approach the clerk, introduce themselves formally, and spell out their first name clearly so the clerk can register them on the roster:
  - *Learner:* "Guten Tag. Ich bin John. Ich komme aus England. Buchstabieren Sie das, bitte? — J-O-H-N. Danke. Auf Wiedersehen!" [Days 1, 5 core, Unit 3]

### 13. Communication-Repair Practice

- If the clerk mishears a letter and writes down the wrong spelling, the learner uses their repair chunk: **"Nein. Noch einmal, bitte."** [Day 1 core, Lesson 4] to repeat the spelling calmly in German.

### 14. Native-Response/Interaction Practice

- *Native (Clerk):* "Guten Tag. Wie heißen Sie?" [Unit 2]
- *Learner:* "Ich heiße John." [Unit 2]
- *Native (Clerk):* "Buchstabieren Sie das, bitte!" [Unit 3]
- *Learner:* "J-O-H-N."
- *Native (Clerk):* "Ja, John. Danke."
- *Learner:* "Danke. Auf Wiedersehen!" [Days 1, 5 core]

### 15. Optional Interest-Driven German Exposure

- Find a children's spelling game or nursery rhyme video teaching the German alphabet online. Sing along to consolidate the letter names and vowel distinctions [Unit 3].

### 16. Mastery Check

- **Can-Do Checklist:**
  - Can formally bid farewell using *Auf Wiedersehen* [Day 5 core].
  - Can spell their first name in German without hesitation [Unit 3].
  - Can accurately transcribe spelled German words containing the tricky letter contrasts *E* and *I* [Unit 3].

---

### WEEK 1, DAY 5 TIME BUDGET ALLOCATION (Total: \~4 Hours)

- **A. Daily German Core:** 15 minutes (Reviewing previous items, adding *Auf Wiedersehen*) [Day 5 core].
- **B. Main Curriculum Lesson:** 45 minutes (Alphabet learning, phonetic spelling drills, administrative registration vocabulary) [Unit 3].
- **C. Intensive Practice & Drills:** 2.5 hours (Drilling alphabet sounds, transcribing native spelling audios, writing and spelling German city names).
- **D. Real-Life German / Interest-Driven Exposure:** 30 minutes (Watching alphabet learning videos, spelling out household items using German letter names) [3Third, 322].

---

## DAY 6: Mobile coordinates and spatial tracking (*hier / dort*)

### 1. Day Objective

The learner will be able to exchange mobile phone numbers, explain spatial coordinates using the adverbs *hier* (here) and *dort* (there), and use the high-frequency neuter noun *das Handy* in conversation.

### 2. Main Curriculum Lesson Content

- **INTRODUCE & UNDERSTAND:** The teacher introduces the adverbs *hier* and *dort* and the noun *das Handy*[Day 6 core, Unit 6]. The student learns how to use spatial coordinates to locate personal belongings [Unit 6].
- **RECOGNIZE:** The learner listens to instructions and points to corresponding locations on a blueprint map based on whether the speaker says *hier* or *dort* [Unit 6].
- **RETRIEVE:** Retrieval of numbers 0–20 to exchange phone coordinates.
- **CONTROLLED USE:** Prompt-response drills matching spatial indicators with nearby or distant objects.
- **SENTENCE BUILDING:** See Section 10.
- **CONTEXTUAL USE:** Partners point to items on their study desks and ask each other where they are located [Unit 6].
- **REAL-LIFE USE:** Pointing out office facilities or transcribing contact numbers at an administrative counter.
- **REVISIT:** Quick spelling audit of *Handy* and *hier* before finishing.

### 3. Daily German Core Items

- **Das Handy** (Mobile phone / Neuter Noun) [Day 6 core]
- **Hier / Dort** (Here / There / Spatial adverbs) [Day 6 core]

### 4. Pronunciation Practice

- **Focus:** The vocalic **"r"** ending in *hier* and the dental consonant **"d"** in *dort* [Unit 6].
- **Progression:**
  1. *Sound:* /d/ (Clean voiced alveolar stop—pronounced with the tongue tip pressing against the upper gums, avoiding any heavy aspiration).
  2. *Syllable:* /dɔʁt/
  3. *Word:* *dort* [dɔɐ̯t]
  4. *Phrase:* *dort ist...* [dɔɐ̯t ɪst]
  5. *Sentence:* *Das Handy ist dort.* [das ˈhɛndi ɪst dɔɐ̯t]
  6. *Connected Speech:* *Wo ist das Handy? — Das Handy ist dort.* (Contrast with *hier* [hi:ɐ̯] where the final "r" is a soft schwa-breath [Unit 11]).

### 5. Minimum Theory/Explanation

- **Spatial Coordinates:** German uses simple spatial adverbs to show distance from the speaker [Unit 6]:
  - **hier:** Located near the speaker ("here") [Day 6 core].
  - **dort:** Located away from the speaker ("there / over there") [Day 6 core].
- **V2 Structure with Spatial Adverbs:**
  - When stating where an item is, the verb *ist* remains locked in Position 2 [Unit 6]: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c} \text{Car 1: Subject} & \text{Car 2: Verb (Locked)} & \text{Car 3: Spatial Adverb} \ \hline \text{Das Handy} & \text{ist} & \text{hier.} \ \text{Herr Schmidt} & \text{ist} & \text{dort.} \end{array}$$

### 6. Listening Practice

- **Bottom-Up Decoding:** Listen to three native speakers locating objects [Unit 6]. Identify whether the speaker says *hier* or *dort* by checking the corresponding box on a worksheet.
- **Number Transcription:** Listen to a speaker dictating a 10-digit mobile number in German connected speech. Write down the digits exactly as you hear them.

### 7. Speaking Practice

- **Physical Alignment Drill:** Touch your phone and say: *Das Handy ist hier.* Point to a distant desk and say: *Das Handy ist dort.*
- **Number Dictation:** Read out your own phone number (or a simulated 10-digit number) in German to a partner using clear pronunciation for each digit.

### 8. Reading Practice

- The student reads a simple office directory sheet [Unit 6]:
  - *Computer: Raum 101 (hier)*
  - *Drucker: Raum 202 (dort)*
- The student summarizes aloud: *Der Computer ist hier. Der Drucker ist dort.*

### 9. Writing Practice

- **Guided Composition:** Write a short status update message to a colleague:
  - `Guten Tag. Ich bin bei der Arbeit.` [Day 15 corrected core]
  - `Mein Handy ist hier.`
  - `Der Drucker ist dort.` [Day 26 core]

### 10. Mandatory Daily Sentence-Building

- **Task:** Spatial Adverb Substitutions.
- **Execution:** Create parallel location statements by swapping nouns and spatial adverbs:
  - *Das Handy ist \_\_\_\_\_\_ (here).* $\rightarrow$ *Das Handy ist ****hier****.*
  - *Der Drucker ist \_\_\_\_\_\_ (there).* $\rightarrow$ *Der Drucker ist ****dort****.*
- **Mini-Dialogue Creation:** Ask and answer location questions:
  - *Wo ist das Handy?* $\rightarrow$ *Das Handy ist hier.*
  - *Wer ist dort?* $\rightarrow$ *Frau Müller ist dort.*

### 11. Retrieval/Review

- Retrieve the spelling skills from Day 5 to spell out the word *Handy* or your name when asked during the practical task.

### 12. Practical Germany Task

- **The Lost Belongings Query:** The learner roleplays looking for their lost mobile phone at a training center reception desk. They must greet the clerk formally, explain that their phone is missing, and locate it spatially when pointed out by the clerk:
  - *Learner:* "Guten Tag. Entschuldigung. Wo ist mein Handy? — Ist das Handy dort? — Ja, das Handy ist hier. Danke!" [Days 1, 4, 6 core, Unit 6]

### 13. Communication-Repair Practice

- If the clerk points to an incorrect item, the learner immediately uses: **"Nein. Ich verstehe nicht. Noch einmal, bitte."** [Days 1, 3 core, Lesson 4] to redirect the search in German.

### 14. Native-Response/Interaction Practice

- *Native (Clerk):* "Guten Tag. Ist das Ihr Handy?"
- *Learner:* "Wie bitte?" [Day 3 core]
- *Native (Clerk):* "Ist... das... Ihr... Handy?"
- *Learner:* "Ja! Das Handy ist hier. Danke." [Day 6 core]
- *Native (Clerk):* "Bitte schön. Tschüss!" [Day 2 core]
- *Learner:* "Tschüss!" [Day 2 core]

### 15. Optional Interest-Driven German Exposure

- Watch a short, simple German commercial or product review of a smartphone online. Listen for how the presenter says the word *Handy* and notice their spatial gestures [3Third].

### 16. Mastery Check

- **Can-Do Checklist:**
  - Can use the spatial adverbs *hier* and *dort* accurately to point out locations [Day 6 core].
  - Can transcribe a spoken 10-digit phone number with basic numerical accuracy.
  - Can state "Das Handy ist hier" with correct V2 syntactic order [Day 6 core].

---

### WEEK 1, DAY 6 TIME BUDGET ALLOCATION (Total: \~4 Hours)

- **A. Daily German Core:** 15 minutes (Reviewing previous items, adding *Das Handy*, *Hier/Dort*) [Day 6 core].
- **B. Main Curriculum Lesson:** 45 minutes (Spatial grammar introduction, V2 location structures, contact coordinate exchange) [Unit 6].
- **C. Intensive Practice & Drills:** 2.5 hours (Exchanging simulated phone numbers, point-and-say spatial drills, transcribing spoken digits).
- **D. Real-Life German / Interest-Driven Exposure:** 30 minutes (Watching German tech reviews, practicing self-talk locating objects around the room) [3Third, 322].

---

## DAY 7: Situational Synthesis, Spaced Review, and formal checkout

### 1. Day Objective

The learner will be able to synthesize all Week 1 grammatical and phonetic skills to conduct a complete, formal introductory checkout roleplay, transcribing personal details and resolving communication barriers entirely in German.

### 2. Main Curriculum Lesson Content

- **INTRODUCE & UNDERSTAND:** The teacher reviews all Week 1 structural baselines, focusing on S-V-O default structures vs. the absolute V2 position 2 rule [Unit 1]. The student learns how to consolidate spelling, greeting registers, and spatial coordinates into a single, cohesive communicative flow [Unit 40].
- **RECOGNIZE:** The learner scans a comprehensive written dialogue and highlights any V2 violations or register inconsistencies.
- **RETRIEVE:** Systematic spaced retrieval of all vocabulary and interaction formulas from Days 1–6.
- **CONTROLLED USE:** Restructuring scrambled statements to start with either subjects or greetings while keeping the verb locked in Position 2.
- **SENTENCE BUILDING:** See Section 10.
- **CONTEXTUAL USE:** Partner work executing a multi-stage registration mixer from start to finish.
- **REAL-LIFE USE:** Approaching a formal checkout examiner to complete the Week 1 milestone interaction task.
- **REVISIT:** Final pronunciation audit of vowel lengths and consonant releases before the test.

### 3. Daily German Core Items

- **Ich komme aus...** (I come from... / Sentence Frame) [Day 7 core]

### 4. Pronunciation Practice

- **Focus:** Connected speech rhythm, **liaison**, and **terminal devoicing** across all Week 1 target sounds [Unit 1, 40].
- **Progression:**
  1. *Sound:* /ç/ $\rightarrow$ /v/ $\rightarrow$ /z/ $\rightarrow$ /ts/ (Reviewing all smiling hushing sounds, dental buzzing, and affricates).
  2. *Syllable:* *ich bin* $\rightarrow$ *kommen aus* (Practice sliding word boundaries smoothly without choppy pauses).
  3. *Word:* *Guten Tag* [gu\:tən ta\:k] (Notice the terminal "g" devoices to a "k" sound).
  4. *Phrase:* *Ich komme aus England.*
  5. *Sentence:* *Guten Tag. Ich bin John, ich komme aus England.*
  6. *Connected Speech:* Deliver the entire introductory block as a single, connected breathing unit with natural rising and falling sentence stress.

### 5. Minimum Theory/Explanation

- **The Absolute V2 Anchor:** German sentences can start with different elements (a greeting, a pronoun, a subject). Regardless of what sits in Car 1, the conjugated verb **must remain locked in Car 2 (Position 2)**: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c} \text{Car 1: Subject/Greeting} & \text{Car 2: Verb (Locked)} & \text{Car 3: Modifier/Object} \ \hline \text{Ich} & \text{bin} & \text{John.} \ \text{Sie} & \text{sind} & \text{Herr Wagner.} \ \text{Woher} & \text{kommen} & \text{Sie?} \end{array}$$ *S-V-O is a reliable starting default, but V2 is the absolute structural law of the German main clause.*

### 6. Listening Practice

- **Bottom-Up Connected Transcription:** Listen to a native dialogue spoken at normal conversational speed. Transcribe the key details (Name, Origin, Language, Phone number) exactly as they are spoken, utilizing bottom-up word boundary parsing.
- **Register Audit:** Listen to three different interactions. Identify whether each interaction is formal (*Sie*) or informal (*du*) based on the pronoun-verb agreement heard.

### 7. Speaking Practice

- **The Mirror Self-Presentation:** Speak a continuous 5-sentence formal introduction aloud in front of a mirror, maintaining correct mouth postures, tongue placements, and natural sentence stress.
- **Rapid Question Response:** Answer verbal prompts from the teacher immediately in German:
  - *Teacher:* "Woher kommen Sie?" $\rightarrow$ *Learner:* "Ich komme aus England."

### 8. Reading Practice

- The student reads a short, un-simplified dialogue transcript from a registration mixer:
  - *Guten Tag. Ich bin David Wagner. Ich komme aus Kanada. Ich spreche Englisch und ein bisschen Deutsch. Mein Handy ist hier. Und Sie sind Herr Schmidt? Danke.*
- The learner verifies that the verbs are in Position 2 in every main clause.

### 9. Writing Practice

- **Controlled Essay Composition:** The learner writes their own formal introduction paragraph (5 sentences) in their notebook, ensuring correct noun capitalization, punctuation, and perfect verb-second syntax.

### 10. Mandatory Daily Sentence-Building

- **Task:** The Mini-Dialogue Creation (Synthesis).
- **Execution:** Construct a complete, coherent conversation between a traveler and an administrator, utilizing the entire Week 1 language toolkit:
  - *Traveler:* "Guten Tag. Ich bin John."
  - *Clerk:* "Guten Tag, Herr John. Woher kommen Sie?"
  - *Traveler:* "Ich komme aus England. Sprechen Sie Englisch?"
  - *Clerk:* "Ein bisschen."
  - *Traveler:* "Danke! Tschüss!"

### 11. Retrieval/Review

- This day is entirely dedicated to systematic review and consolidation of Days 1–6.

### 12. Practical Germany Task

- **The Course Registration checkout:** The learner roleplays approaching an administrator at a German training school center [Unit 3]. They must greet them formally, state their name and country of origin, ask if the clerk speaks English, spell their name when requested, and locate their registration document spatially:
  - *Learner:* "Guten Tag. Ich bin John. Ich komme aus England. Sprechen Sie Englisch? — Buchstabieren Sie das, bitte. — J-O-H-N. — Mein Dokument ist dort. Danke. Auf Wiedersehen!" [Days 1, 3, 5, 6, 7 core, Unit 3]

### 13. Communication-Repair Practice

- If the administrator speaks too rapidly or mumbles, the learner must immediately deploy: **"Wie bitte? Langsam, bitte!"** [Day 3 core] to slow down the input and keep the interaction active in German.

### 14. Native-Response/Interaction Practice

- *Native (Examiner):* "Guten Tag. Wer ist das?"
- *Learner:* "Guten Tag. Ich bin John. Ich komme aus England." [Day 7 core]
- *Native (Examiner):* "Sprechen Sie Deutsch?"
- *Learner:* "Ein bisschen. Ich lerne heute." [Day 3 core]
- *Native (Examiner):* "Sehr gut. Woher kommen Sie?"
- *Learner:* "Ich komme aus England. Danke." [Day 7 core]
- *Native (Examiner):* "Auf Wiedersehen!" [Day 5 core]
- *Learner:* "Auf Wiedersehen!" [Day 5 core]

### 15. Optional Interest-Driven German Exposure

- Review a simplified German cartoon or online vlog. Try to map and outline how greetings, self-introductions, and spelling are naturally used by the speakers in different social registers [Unit 1].

### 16. Mastery Check

- The learner successfully completes the Day 7 Milestone Task under un-speeded roleplay conditions, demonstrating V2 syntax stability, clear phonetic boundaries, and spontaneous repair deployment [Unit 40].

---

### WEEK 1, DAY 7 TIME BUDGET ALLOCATION (Total: \~4 Hours)

- **A. Daily German Core:** 20 minutes (Reviewing all Days 1–7 items) [Day 7 core].
- **B. Main Curriculum Lesson:** 40 minutes (Verb-Second syntactic review, S-V-O default comparison, dialogue building) [Unit 1, 40].
- **C. Intensive Practice & Drills:** 2.5 hours (Mirror presentation, question-answer conversion drills, spelling and phone number dictations, mock testing).
- **D. Real-Life German / Interest-Driven Exposure:** 30 minutes (Reviewing German cartoons or vlogs, conducting a final self-assessment checklist) [3Third, 322].

# WEEK 1 REVIEW

### 1. What should now be recognized (Receptive Mastery)

- The structural difference between formal *Sie* and informal *du* registers in social scenarios [Unit 11].
- The W-Question words *Wer*, *Wie*, *Was*, and *Woher* as inquiries about identity, name, and origin [Unit 2, 3].
- Tricky German alphabet letter contrasts (*E* vs. *I*, *A* vs. *H*, *J* vs. *Y*, *Z* vs. "z") in connected spelling audios [Unit 3].
- The adverbs *hier* and *dort* indicating spatial location [Unit 6].
- Bottom-up word boundaries and connected speech reductions (e.g., how *Ich bin* or *Sie sind* slide together smoothly) [Unit 1, 11].

### 2. What should now be actively produced (Active Mastery)

- Formal greetings (*Guten Tag*) and informal partings (*Tschüss*) [Day 1, 2 core].
- Formal self-identity statements using *Ich bin [Name]* and acknowledging partners formally using *Sie sind...* [Unit 1].
- Present-tense singular of the verb *sein* (*ich bin*, *Sie sind*, *er/sie/es ist*) with correct pronoun agreement [Unit 11].
- Asking and answering yes/no questions (*Sprechen Sie Englisch? — Ja, ein bisschen.*) [Day 3 core].
- Stating origin using *Ich komme aus...* and asking *Woher kommen Sie?* with correct regular suffix inflections [Day 7 core, Unit 3].
- Spelling first names accurately using German letter names [Unit 3].
- Exchanging 10-digit mobile phone numbers and pointing out items spatially using *hier* and *dort* [Day 6 core].
- Deploying repair and clarification formulas (*Wie bitte?*, *Langsam, bitte!*) to slow down rapid native inputs [Day 3 core].

### 3. What should remain mainly receptive

- Full present-tense conjugation paradigms for other verbs or plural pronouns (*wir*, *ihr*, *sie* plural) [Unit 11, 12].
- Noun gender endings and case markings (*Akkusativ/Dativ*) beyond fixed, frozen chunks [Unit 13, 26].
- Complex spelling of foreign or technical country names [Unit 3].

### 4. Practical Abilities Gained

- Can navigate a formal check-in lobby, introducing themselves and stating their origin in correct German [Unit 1].
- Can spell out their name clearly to an administrative clerk or registrar [Unit 3].
- Can request an interlocutor to repeat or slow down rapid speech, keeping the conversation entirely in German [Day 3 core].
- Can exchange contact details and describe the relative locations of workspace tools (*hier / dort*) [Day 6 core].

### 5. Weaknesses to Check (Diagnostic Guide)

- **V2 Violations:** Check if the learner is placing the verb at the end of statements (e.g., *Ich heute hier bin*) or using temporal inversion prematurely (e.g., *Guten Tag, Herr Schmidt, bin ich John*). The verb must sit firmly in Position 2.
- **Pronunciation defaults:** Ensure the learner is not substituting the English "w" for German /v/ in *wer*, or English "sh" for the smiling palatal hissing "ich-laut" (/ç/) in *ich*, *nicht*, and *spreche* [Unit 1, 318].
- **Capitalization errors:** Check that the learner consistently capitalizes *Ich*, all German nouns (*Handy*, *Supermarkt*, *Bahnhof*), and formal address pronouns (*Sie*) [Day 6, 8 core, Unit 11].

---

# WEEK 1 MASTERY CHECK

This formal evaluation uses **qualitative, functional competence criteria** to determine readiness for Week 2, completely bypassing arbitrary speed, percentage, or trial-count gates [Newton & Nation, 2020].

```
+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|                                 WEEK 1 MASTERY CHECKLIST                                        |
+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|  1. PHONOLOGICAL INTELLIGIBILITY (Receptive & Productive)                                       |
|     -> Can produce distinct smiling /ç/ ("ich-laut") and dental buzzing /v/ sounds.             |
|                                                                                                 |
|  2. CANONICAL V2 STABILITY                                                                      |
|     -> Keeps the conjugated verb locked in Position 2 across statements and W-questions.        |
|                                                                                                 |
|  3. PORTABLE REPAIR ALIGNMENT (The Safety Net)                                                  |
|     -> Spontaneously deploys "Wie bitte?" and "Langsam, bitte!" during communication breaks.   |
|                                                                                                 |
|  4. LOGISTICAL ADMINISTRATIVE AUTONOMY (TBLT Task)                                              |
|     -> Greet, state name, state origin, spell name, exchange number, and bid goodbye formally.  |
+-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

```

### 1. Pronunciation Check

- **Target Sound Isolation:** The learner reads aloud the following three sentences, which contain the critical phonetic contrasts of Week 1 [Vaughan-Rees, 2002]:
  1. *Guten Tag. Ich bin Wer-ner.* (Evaluates German initial /v/ in *Wer-ner* and palatal fricative /ç/ in *ich*).
  2. *Ich spre-che ein biss-chen Deutsch.* (Evaluates palatal sibilant /ç/ in *spreche* and *bisschen*, and the /ʃ/ in *Deutsch*).
  3. *Tschüss! Mein Han-dy ist dort.* (Evaluates informal terminal /s/ in *Tschüss*, the /d/ in *dort*, and vowel lengths).
- **Standard of Success:** Intelligible pronunciation. The learner must show a clear articulatory effort to place their tongue forward against the lower teeth for the "ich-laut" (/ç/) and avoid English "w" slurs.

### 2. Functional Communication Check (The Bürgeramt Interview)

- **The Scenario:** The learner approaches the examiner (roleplaying as a busy registrar clerk at a municipal desk in Germany) [Unit 3].
- **The Task:** The learner must complete a 6-step interactive cycle under natural, un-speeded conditions:
  1. Initiate the contact with a formal greeting (*Guten Tag*) [Day 1 core].
  2. Apologize politely using the core formula (*Entschuldigung*) [Day 4 core].
  3. Introduce themselves formally (*Ich bin [Name]*) [Unit 1].
  4. State country of origin (*Ich komme aus [Land]*) [Day 7 core].
  5. Inquire if the clerk speaks English, and spell out their name clearly when requested [Day 3 core, Unit 3].
  6. Locate their registration document or phone spatially using coordinates (*hier / dort*), and bid farewell formally (*Auf Wiedersehen*) [Day 5, 6 core].
- **Standard of Success:** Conversational success. Grammatical slips (such as minor suffix omissions) are recast implicitly [Long, 2015]. The learner meets the criteria if meaning is successfully negotiated, the verb remains consistently in Position 2, and any rapid input is slowed down using German repair formulas (*Wie bitte?*, *Langsam, bitte!*) without reverting to English.

---

*The complete Week 1 (Days 1–7) detailed study plan is finalized, audited, and officially locked under the designated design parameters. Do not proceed to Week 2.*

# WEEK 2 — DAYS 8–14


## DAY 8: Expanding Presence/Absence, Location Status, and the Bahnhof Baseline

### 1. Day Objective

The learner will be able to state the presence or absence of core workplace and public facilities using *sein* + *da / nicht da*, declare their place of residence using the verb *wohnen* in the first person singular, and identify local transit landmarks (*der Bahnhof*).

### 2. Main Curriculum Lesson Content

- **INTRODUCE:** The instructor presents the postpositional presence markers *da* (here/present) and *nicht da* (not here/absent) [a1-master-curriculum-v2.md], alongside the regular verb *wohnen* (to reside) [Unit 12].
- **UNDERSTAND:** The learner reads the explicit declarative layout explaining how *da* and *nicht da* follow the copula *ist/sind*. They observe that the verb remains locked in the V2 Position 2 anchor [Unit 1].
- **RECOGNIZE:** The learner listens to short local audio clips and marks whether the target landmark (*der Bahnhof*) or person is declared present (*da*) or absent (*nicht da*) [Unit 11].
- **RETRIEVE:** The learner retrieves the formal pronouns and greetings from Week 1 to frame the statements.
- **CONTROLLED USE:** Gap-fill exercises matching subject nouns with *ist da* or *ist nicht da*.
- **SENTENCE BUILDING:** See Section 10.
- **CONTEXTUAL USE:** Partner drill where students use office diagrams to state whether specific resources (e.g., *der Drucker*, *das Handy*) are present at their desks [Unit 6].
- **REAL-LIFE USE:** Arriving at a train station info counter and confirming if a specific transit office or coordinator is present.
- **REVISIT:** Fast vocal rehearsal of the long closed vowel in *wohnen* [Unit 12].

### 3. Daily German Core Items

- **der Bahnhof** (The train station / Masculine Noun) [Day 8 core]
- **Ich wohne in...** (I live in... / Sentence Frame) [Day 8 core]

### 4. Pronunciation Practice

- **Focus:** The silent **"h"** and long closed **"o"** (/o:/) in *wohnen* vs. the short open **"o"** in *dort* [Unit 12].
- **Progression:**
  1. *Sound:* /o:/ (Tense, highly rounded lips, tongue pulled back—similar to the vowel in English "boat" but produced as a pure monophthong without the "w" glide at the end).
  2. *Word:* *wohnen* [ˈvo\:nən] (The "h" is completely silent, serving only to mark vowel length) [Unit 12].
  3. *Phrase:* *Ich wohne in...* [ɪç ˈvo\:nə ɪn].
  4. *Sentence:* *Ich wohne in Berlin.*
  5. *Connected Speech:* *Guten Tag. Ich bin John. Ich wohne in Berlin.* (Smooth liaison without glottal stops between *wohne* and *in*).

### 5. Minimum Theory/Explanation

- **Existential and Location Status:** To declare whether an object, landmark, or person is present or absent, German utilizes the verb *sein* (to be) paired with the adverbs **da** (present/there) or **nicht da** (absent/not there) [a1-master-curriculum-v2.md].
- **The V2 Anchor:** The verb remains locked in Position 2, while the subject occupies Position 1 and the status marker sits in Position 3 [Unit 1]: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c} \text{Position 1 (Subject)} & \text{Position 2 (Verb: Locked)} & \text{Position 3 (Status)} \ \hline \text{Der Bahnhof} & \text{ist} & \text{da.} \ \text{Herr Schmidt} & \text{ist} & \text{nicht da.} \end{array}$$

### 6. Listening Practice

- **Bottom-Up Decoding:** Listen to five native German speakers stating their place of residence. Identify how the terminal schwa in *wohne* (/ə/) naturally blends into the preposition *in* (/ɪn/), creating a unified acoustic transition: /ˈvo\:nə.ɪn/ [Unit 12].
- **Acoustic Status Identification:** Listen to an office coordinate audio track. Circle whether the speaker says *da* or *nicht da* for the target resources.

### 7. Speaking Practice

- **Oral Substitution:** The instructor prompts: *"Drucker"* \\(\rightarrow\\) Learner says: *Der Drucker ist da.* [Day 26 core]. *"Schlüssel"* \\(\rightarrow\\) Learner says: *Der Schlüssel ist nicht da.* [Unit 11].
- **Connected Self-Presentation:** Speak a fluid three-sentence block: *Hallo. Ich bin David. Ich wohne in Frankfurt.*[Day 8 core].

### 8. Reading Practice

- The learner scans a simple printed office status list [Unit 11]:
  - *Der Bahnhof ist dort. Frau Müller ist da. Herr Wagner ist nicht da.*
- The student highlights the verb *ist* in each line to visually reinforce its Position 2 anchor [Unit 1].

### 9. Writing Practice

- **Controlled Composition:** Complete the short status log by writing the correct copula and presence marker:
  1. `Frau Müller ist heute da.` [Unit 11]
  2. `Der Bahnhof ist dort.` [Day 8 core]

### 10. Mandatory Daily Sentence-Building Exercise

- **Task:** Noun to Statement Expansion [Day 8 core].
- **Execution:** Convert single nouns into complete, grammatically correct location and status statements using *sein*:
  - *der Bahnhof* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Der Bahnhof ist dort.* [Day 8 core]
  - *das Handy* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Das Handy ist da.* [Day 6 core]
  - *der Schlüssel* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Der Schlüssel ist nicht da.* [Unit 11]

### 11. Retrieval from Week 1

- The learner explicitly retrieves the German alphabet letter names from Week 1 to spell their place of residence (*F-R-A-N-K-F-U-R-T*) aloud [Unit 3].

### 12. Practical Germany Task

- **The Info Desk Coordinate Check:** The learner roleplays approaching an information kiosk at a German train station (*Bahnhof*) [Day 8 core]. They must greet the clerk formally, state their residence, and ask if their vocational training coordinator (*Herr Schmidt*) is present [Unit 11]:
  - *Learner:* "Guten Tag. Ich bin John. Ich wohne in Frankfurt. Ist Herr Schmidt da? — Nein, Herr Schmidt ist heute nicht da. — Ah, danke. Tschüss!" [Days 1, 2, 8 core, Unit 11]

### 13. Communication Repair

- If the clerk speaks too rapidly, the learner immediately deploys: **"Wie bitte?"** [Day 3 core] with rising intonation, using meaning-focused negotiation rather than reverting to English.

### 14. Realistic Native Response/Interaction

- *Native (Clerk):* "Guten Tag. Wohnen Sie in Berlin?" [Unit 12]
- *Learner:* "Nein, ich wohne in Frankfurt. Ist der Bahnhof hier?" [Day 8 core]
- *Native (Clerk):* "Ja, der Bahnhof ist direkt dort." [Day 8 core]
- *Learner:* "Vielen Dank! Tschüss!" [Day 2 core]

### 15. Optional Interest-Driven German Exposure

- **The Spaced Retrieval Booster:** The learner spends 15 minutes reviewing their personal digital flashcard deck (containing all Week 1 items and today's new nouns). They use active recall (producing the word-form before flipping the card) to consolidate the memory traces.

### 16. Mastery Check

- **Can-Do Checklist:**
  - Can locate the local transit hub (*der Bahnhof*) and state their city of residence (*Ich wohne in...*) [Day 8 core].
  - Can declare presence or absence using *da* and *nicht da* with stable V2 syntax [Unit 11].

---

### WEEK 2, DAY 8 STUDY TIME ALLOCATION (Total: \~4 Hours)

- **A. Daily German Core:** 15 minutes (Focusing on *der Bahnhof*, *Ich wohne in...*) [Day 8 core].
- **B. Main Curriculum Lesson:** 45 minutes (Existential copula, V2 location structures, *wohnen* conjugation) [Unit 12].
- **C. Intensive Practice:** 2.5 hours (30 mins: mouth-muscle workouts for long closed /o:/; 60 mins: physical sentence block-swapping; 60 mins: transcribing native residence recordings).
- **D. Real-Life German:** 30 minutes (Self-narration: describing the location of household items using *hier / dort / da / nicht da*).

## DAY 9: Establishing Possession, Basic Requests, and the Polite Frame (*Können Sie...*)

### 1. Day Objective

The learner will be able to declare possession of essential tools using the verb *haben* in the first person singular and formal address (*ich habe*, *Sie haben*), and formulate polite requests using the pre-assembled frame *Können Sie...* [Day 9 core, Unit 17].

### 2. Main Curriculum Lesson Content

- **INTRODUCE:** The instructor presents the present-tense singular of the transitive verb *haben* (to have) [Unit 17], and the polite auxiliary request frame *Können Sie mir helfen?* (Can you help me?) [Day 9 core].
- **UNDERSTAND:** The learner reads the explicit grammatical breakdown of *haben* conjugation. They observe that *Können Sie...* functions as a locked, holistic request frame that sends the uninflected main verb (*helfen*, *aufschreiben*) to the absolute end of the sentence [279, Unit 21].
- **RECOGNIZE:** The learner listens to short dialogs and identifies whether a speaker is asserting possession (*Ich habe...*) or requesting assistance (*Können Sie...*) [Day 9 core].
- **RETRIEVE:** The learner retrieves spatial adverbs (*hier/dort*) and nouns (*das Handy*) from previous days [Day 6 core].
- **CONTROLLED USE:** Scrambled word-ordering exercises to construct polite queries.
- **SENTENCE BUILDING:** See Section 10.
- **CONTEXTUAL USE:** Partner work where students ask to borrow workstation tools politely using *Können Sie...*[Unit 21].
- **REAL-LIFE USE:** Approaching a school office and requesting an administrator to write down contact details.
- **REVISIT:** Practicing the voiced glottal friction sound /h/ in *haben* [Unit 17].

### 3. Daily German Core Items

- **Schreiben Sie das bitte auf?** (Could you please write that down? / Sentence Frame) [Day 9 core]
- **Können Sie mir helfen?** (Can you help me? / Sentence Frame) [Day 9 core]

### 4. Pronunciation Practice

- **Focus:** The glottal stop and consonant clusters in **"aufschreiben"** (/bu\:xʃtabi:ʁən/ vs. /ˈaʊfʃʁaɪbən/) [Unit 3].
- **Progression:**
  1. *Sound:* /ʃ/ (Voiceless postalveolar fricative—projected, rounded lips blowing air out to make a loud "sh" sound).
  2. *Syllable:* /aʊf/ $\rightarrow$ /ʃʁaɪbən/
  3. *Word:* *aufschreiben* [ˈaʊfʃʁaɪbən] (Write down).
  4. *Phrase:* *Schreiben Sie das auf?* [ˈʃʁaɪbən zi: das aʊf] (Separable verb layout in active syntax).
  5. *Sentence:* *Schreiben Sie das bitte auf?* [ˈʃʁaɪbən zi: das ˈbɪtə aʊf] [Day 9 core].
  6. *Connected Speech:* *Können Sie mir helfen? Schreiben Sie das bitte auf?* [Day 9 core].

### 5. Minimum Theory/Explanation

- **Polite Request Frames (Split Brackets):** To request assistance or make a polite demand in German, we use the modal auxiliary frame **Können Sie...** [Day 9 core]. In Yes/No questions, the auxiliary *Können* sits in Position 1, the subject pronoun *Sie* in Position 2, and the uninflected main verb in the infinitive (ending in *-en*) is sent to the **absolute end of the sentence** [Unit 21]: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c|c} \text{Pos 1: Auxiliary} & \text{Pos 2: Subject} & \text{Pos 3: Recipient/Object} & \text{Pos 4: Infinitive (End)} \ \hline \text{Können} & \text{Sie} & \text{mir bitte} & \text{helfen?} \ \text{Schreiben} & \text{Sie} & \text{das bitte} & \text{auf?} \end{array}$$ *Note: "Schreiben... auf" is a separable verb that split-brackets exactly like a modal frame at this level.*

### 6. Listening Practice

- **Bottom-Up Decoding:** Listen to a native speaker pronouncing *Schreiben Sie das bitte auf?* [Day 9 core]. Identify the brief pause preceding the separable prefix *auf* at the very end of the sentence, noting the terminal voiceless bilabial release /p/.
- **Gist Extraction:** Listen to three short office interactions. Identify which speaker is asking for help by marking the *Können Sie mir helfen?* box.

### 7. Speaking Practice

- **Transformation Drills:** Convert statements into polite requests:
  - *Sie helfen mir.* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Können Sie mir helfen?* [Day 9 core]
  - *Sie schreiben das auf.* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Schreiben Sie das bitte auf?* [Day 9 core]
- **Vocal Rehearsal:** Practice delivering *Können Sie mir helfen?* as a single, smooth connected breath block: /ˈkœnən zi: mi:ɐ̯ ˈhɛlfən/.

### 8. Reading Practice

- The student reads an office assistance request note [Day 9 core]:
  - *Guten Tag. Ich habe ein Handy, aber ich habe den Schlüssel nicht. Können Sie mir helfen? Schreiben Sie das bitte auf? Danke.* [Note: "den Schlüssel" is learned receptively here; the focus is on request syntax].
- The student draws an arrow from the auxiliary at the start to the infinitive at the end of each request.

### 9. Writing Practice

- **Guided Composition:** Write a short request note to a classmate:
  - `Guten Tag. Ich habe das Handy nicht.`
  - `Können Sie mir helfen?` [Day 9 core]
  - `Danke.`

### 10. Mandatory Daily Sentence-Building Exercise

- **Task:** Statement to Question Transformation [Unit 1].
- **Execution:** Swap the subject and verb to convert declarative statements into yes/no questions:
  - *Sie haben das Handy.* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Haben Sie das Handy?* [Day 6 core, Unit 17]
  - *Sie sind Herr Schmidt.* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Sind Sie Herr Schmidt?* [Unit 11]
  - *Sie helfen mir.* \\(\rightarrow\\) *Können Sie mir helfen?* [Day 9 core]

### 11. Retrieval from Week 1

- The learner retrieves the spelling of their first name in German to dictate it to their partner while executing today's writing request [Unit 3].

### 12. Practical Germany Task

- **The Receptionist Query:** The learner roleplays approaching the registrar's desk at their vocational school [Unit 11]. They have lost their contact coordinates and need the clerk to write down the coordinator's number [Day 9 core]:
  - *Learner:* "Guten Tag. Ich bin John. Ich habe das Handy hier. Können Sie mir helfen? Schreiben Sie das bitte auf? Danke!" [Days 1, 6, 9 core, Unit 17]

### 13. Communication Repair

- If the clerk speaks too quickly or uses unfamiliar words, the learner immediately deploys: **"Wie bitte? Langsam, bitte!"** [Day 3 core] to manage the input velocity.

### 14. Realistic Native Response/Interaction

- *Native (Clerk):* "Guten Tag. Haben Sie das Handy?" [Day 6 core]
- *Learner:* "Ja, ich habe das Handy hier. Aber ich habe die Nummer nicht. Können Sie mir helfen?" [Days 6, 9 core]
- *Native (Clerk):* "Ja, kein Problem! Ich schreibe das auf." [Day 13 core]
- *Learner:* "Vielen Dank. Auf Wiedersehen!" [Day 5 core]

### 15. Optional Interest-Driven German Exposure

- **The Input Flood Technique:** The learner prompts an AI language interface: *"Give me 10 natural, short German dialogue sentences using the request frame 'Können Sie...' in everyday situations like offices, cafés, and train stations. Highlight where the end verb sits."*. They read the examples aloud to build intuitive syntax patterns.

### 16. Mastery Check

- **Can-Do Checklist:**
  - Can state possession using *ich habe* and ask questions using *haben Sie* [Unit 17].
  - Can formulate polite requests using the split-bracket frames *Können Sie mir helfen?* and *Schreiben Sie das bitte auf?* with the main verb sent to the end [Day 9 core].

---

### WEEK 2, DAY 9 STUDY TIME ALLOCATION (Total: \~4 Hours)

- **A. Daily German Core:** 20 minutes (Focusing on *Schreiben Sie das bitte auf?*, *Können Sie mir helfen?*) [Day 9 core].
- **B. Main Curriculum Lesson:** 40 minutes (Possession with *haben*, split-bracket modal requesting) [Unit 17, 21].
- **C. Intensive Practice:** 2.5 hours (45 mins: practicing the voiceless sibilant /ʃ/ in *aufschreiben*; 60 mins: Yes/No question transformation drills; 45 mins: roleplaying help-desk interactions).
- **D. Real-Life German:** 30 minutes (Rehearsing spelling and requests aloud while commuting or performing chores).

## DAY 10: Numbers 0–20, Price Decoding, and the Bakery Transaction

### 1. Day Objective

The learner will be able to count from 0 to 20 in German, decode double-digit spoken price totals under un-speeded conditions, and complete a simple retail transaction at a German bakery (*Bäckerei*) [Day 10, 17 core].

### 2. Main Curriculum Lesson Content

- **INTRODUCE:** The instructor presents numbers 0–20 [Unit 4], the currency unit *der Euro* [Day 10 core], and the unanalyzed transaction frame *Das macht...* (That makes...) [Unit 24].
- **UNDERSTAND:** The learner reads the explicit spelling patterns of numbers 1–20. They observe that German uses a stable, uninflected currency singular *Euro* for all prices (e.g., *fünf Euro*, *zwanzig Euro*) [Unit 9, 24].
- **RECOGNIZE:** The learner listens to spoken pricing announcements and transcribes the exact numeric digits [Unit 24].
- **RETRIEVE:** The learner retrieves the retail request frame *Ich möchte..., bitte* (I would like..., please) from Week 1 [Day 17 core].
- **CONTROLLED USE:** Matching number words with digit cards (e.g., *zwanzig* \\(\rightarrow\\) *20*).
- **SENTENCE BUILDING:** See Section 10.
- **CONTEXTUAL USE:** Partner work where students roleplay cashier and customer, scanning bakery items and exchanging cash [Unit 24].
- **REAL-LIFE USE:** Approaching a bakery counter, ordering bread and coffee, and paying with Euro bills.
- **REVISIT:** Focused pronunciation of the German letter sound "Z" (/ts/) in *zwanzig* [Unit 4].

### 3. Daily German Core Items

- **der Euro** (The Euro / Masculine Noun) [Day 10 core]
- **zwanzig** (Twenty / Number) [Day 10 core]

### 4. Pronunciation Practice

- **Focus:** The German alveolar affricate **"Z" (/ts/)** in *zwei* and *zwanzig* [Unit 4].
- **Progression:**
  1. *Sound:* /ts/ (Pronounced like the sharp, voiceless "ts" at the end of the English word "cats" but placed at the start of the word).
  2. *Word:* *zwanzig* [ˈtsvantsɪç] (Note that the "-zig" suffix is pronounced as a soft smiling hushing "ich-laut" [ç]) [Unit 24].
  3. *Phrase:* *zwanzig Euro* [ˈtsvantsɪç ˈaʊʁo].
  4. *Sentence:* *Das macht zwanzig Euro.* [das maxt ˈtsvantsɪç ˈaʊʁo].
  5. *Connected Speech:* *Ich möchte bezahlen, bitte. — Das macht zwanzig Euro.* [Day 10, 23 core].

### 5. Minimum Explanation/Theory

- **Numerical and Price Syntax:** When stating price totals in German, we use the unanalyzed frame **Das macht...**(That makes... / That comes to...) [Unit 24].
- **The Uninflected Currency Rule:** The currency unit *Euro* remains grammatically singular and uninflected when paired with numbers [Unit 9, 24]: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c} \text{Number} & \text{Currency (Uninflected)} & \text{Noun/Item} \ \hline \text{fünf} & \text{Euro} & \text{Brot} \ \text{zwanzig} & \text{Euro} & \text{Kaffee} \end{array}$$

### 6. Listening Practice

- **Bottom-Up Numeric Transcription:** Listen to a fast-paced recording of a German cashier calling out price totals. Write down the numbers as digits (e.g., hearing *fünfzehn Euro* \\(\rightarrow\\) write down *15 €*) [Unit 4].
- **Number Identification:** Listen to spoken number sequences and circle the odd one out (e.g., *zwei — drei — zwei*).

### 7. Speaking Practice

- **Price Generation Drill:** Look at price tags on a worksheet (e.g., *3 €, 12 €, 20 €*) and state aloud: *Das macht drei Euro.*, *Das macht zwölf Euro.*, *Das macht zwanzig Euro.*
- **The Cashier Counter:** Practice reciting the cash transaction dialogue with natural intonation.

### 8. Reading Practice

- The student reads a simple printed receipt layout from a bakery [Unit 24]:
  - *1 x Brot: 4 Euro.*
  - *1 x Kaffee: 3 Euro.*
  - *Gesamt (Total): 7 Euro.*
- The student reads the receipt details aloud in German to verify their pricing comprehension.

### 9. Writing Practice

- **Controlled Number Writing:** Write out the German words for the following digits:
  1. `8` \\(\rightarrow\\) *acht*
  2. `14` \\(\rightarrow\\) *vierzehn*
  3. `20` \\(\rightarrow\\) *zwanzig*

### 10. Mandatory Daily Sentence-Building Exercise

- **Task:** Sentence Transformation (Pricing and Purchases) [Unit 24].
- **Execution:** Build statement-response pairs by combining purchase requests with price totals:
  - `Brot` / `Kaffee` / `Ich möchte` \\(\rightarrow\\) *Ich möchte Brot und Kaffee, bitte.* [Day 17 core]
  - `Das` / `Euro` / `sieben` / `macht` \\(\rightarrow\\) *Das macht sieben Euro.* [Unit 24]
  - `zehn` / `Hier` / `Euro` / `sind` \\(\rightarrow\\) *Hier sind zehn Euro. Stimmt so!* [Day 19, 23 core]

### 11. Retrieval from Week 1

- The learner retrieves the bakery items (*Brot*, *Kaffee*) [Day 17 core] and the politeness response (*Stimmt so!*) [Day 23 core] from Week 1 to populate the transaction dialogue.

### 12. Practical Germany Task

- **The Bakery Run:** The learner roleplays buying breakfast at a German bakery (*Bäckerei*) [Unit 24]. They approach the clerk, order bread and coffee formally, decode the spoken price total up to 20 Euro, hand over cash, and execute polite tipping etiquette [Day 10, 17, 19, 23 core]:
  - *Learner:* "Guten Tag. Ich möchte ein Brot und einen Kaffee, bitte. — Das macht sieben Euro. — Hier sind zehn Euro. Stimmt so! Danke. Tschüss!" [Days 1, 2, 10, 17, 19, 23 core, Unit 24]

### 13. Communication Repair

- If the cashier calls out an unexpected total or asks about loyalty cards, the learner uses: **"Wie bitte? Noch einmal, bitte."** [Days 1, 3 core] to manage the breakdown calmly.

### 14. Realistic Native Response/Interaction

- *Native (Cashier):* "Guten Tag. Bitte schön?"
- *Learner:* "Ich möchte einen Kaffee, bitte." [Day 17 core]
- *Native (Cashier):* "Das macht drei Euro." [Day 10 core]
- *Learner:* "Hier sind fünf Euro. Stimmt so!" [Day 19, 23 core]
- *Native (Cashier):* "Vielen Dank. Tschüss!" [Day 2 core]

### 15. Optional Interest-Driven German Exposure

- **The Virtual Reality Mingle:** If available, the learner watches a short 3D immersive video of a German café checkout. They practice visually tracking the cashier's mouth movements and gestures to build stronger multimodal memory traces.

### 16. Mastery Check

- **Can-Do Checklist:**
  - Can count from 0 to 20 in German with accurate pronunciation of the affricate /ts/ [Unit 4].
  - Can successfully execute a cash bakery transaction, decoding price totals and executing standard tipping etiquette (*Stimmt so!*) [Day 23 core, Unit 24].

---

### WEEK 2, DAY 10 STUDY TIME ALLOCATION (Total: \~4 Hours)

- **A. Daily German Core:** 15 minutes (Focusing on *der Euro*, *zwanzig*) [Day 10 core].
- **B. Main Curriculum Lesson:** 45 minutes (German numbering system, uninflected currency rules, cashier dialogue) [Unit 4, 24].
- **C. Intensive Practice:** 2.5 hours (45 mins: practicing "Z" /ts/ sibilant releases; 60 mins: transcribing rapid price audios; 45 mins: roleplaying cash transactions).
- **D. Real-Life German:** 30 minutes (Rehearsing counting and pricing household items aloud in Euro).
